Simultaneous Decoding of Classical Coset Codes over 3 − 3- User Quantum Interference Channel : New Achievable Rate Regions
Fatma Gouiaa and Arun Padakandla

Abstract
We undertake a Shannon theoretic study of the problem of communicating bit streams over a 3-user classical-quantum interference channel ( 3 − 3- CQIC) and focus on characterizing inner bounds. We design a new coding strategy based on (i) coset codes possessing algebraic closure properties and (ii) decoding POVMs to decode bi-variate interference efficiently. Needing to perform simultaneous decoding, we enhance Sen’s powerful technique of tilting, smoothing, and augmentation - originally designed only for IID codes - to decode into ‘functions of codebooks’. Developing analysis techniques to combine all of these elements, we derive a new inner bound to the capacity region of a 3 − 3- CQIC. The derived inner bound subsumes all currently known inner bounds and is analytically proven to be strictly larger for identified examples including non-commutative ‘additive’ and ‘non-additive’ ones.



I
Introduction

Consider a
3-
user classical-quantum interference channel (
3-
CQIC) shared amidst three distributed transmitter-receiver (Tx-Rx) pairs depicted in Fig.
1
. The three Txs wish to communicate their bit streams reliably to their corresponding Rxs. We undertake a Shannon-theoretic study, and the problem of interest is to characterize the capacity region of the
3-
CQIC. Our specific focus is on designing coding strategies, analyzing their performance, and deriving inner bounds to the capacity of the
3-
CQIC. Let us begin by discussing the current known best coding strategy and the corresponding largest known inner bound.

The best known coding strategy and the largest known inner bound to the capacity region of the
3-
CQIC is based on our knowledge in regards to the
2-
user version, the
2-
CQIC. For any
2-
user interference channel (IC), classical or quantum, the best known coding strategy is based on Han and Kobayashi’s technique
[
1
]
of message splitting via superposition coding, designed in the context of a
2-
user classical IC. Adopting their
[
1
]
strategy, Sen, in his earlier work
[
2
]
, proposed sequential decoding, designed the necessary decoding POVMs, proposed a novel non-commutative union bound
[
3
,
2
]
and proved achievability of the current known largest inner bound for the capacity of a
2-
CQIC in the asymptotic regime. More recently, inventing new techniques of
tilting smoothing and augmentation
(TSA)
[
4
]
- a topic we shall dwell on later - Sen
[
5
]
has designed new simultaneous decoding POVMs, analyzed its performance to derive an inner bound
[
6
]
in the far more challenging and general one-shot regime. Sen’s inner bounds in
[
2
]
and
[
6
]
are indeed the classical-quantum (CQ) generalization of Han and Kobayashi’s inner bound
[
1
]
. In summary, the best known coding strategy for a
2-
CQIC is message splitting via superposition coding
[
1
]
and the corresponding inner bounds are proven achievable by Sen’s simultaneous
[
5
]
and sequential decoding
[
2
]
. When we graduate to the
3-
CQIC, each Tx can also employ Marton’s precoding
[
7
]
to optimize multiplexing of different message parts to enable efficient interference decoding. Thus, one can combine, at least in principle, the techniques of
[
1
]
,
[
7
]
, Sen’s simultaneous decoding POVMs
[
5
]
, his analysis steps
[
5
]
with convex split
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
- the one-shot version of covering
[
13
]
to analyze precoding - and prove achievability of a natural generalization of Sen’s inner bound to the
3-
CQIC both in the one-shot and asymptotic regime. Is this natural generalization of Sen’s inner bound optimal for a general
3-
CQIC?

Fig. 1:
Three bit streams have to be communicated from three distributed Txs to three distributed Rxs over a
3-
CQIC.

One common thread connecting all the above strategies and, more broadly, information-theoretic studies is the all-pervasive use of (unstructured)
independent and identically distributed
(IID) codes. Specifically, in designing coding strategies and proving inner bounds, the multiple codes employed in the strategy and each of their individual codewords are picked IID. Since the highly probable codes that dominate the average performance are
bereft of any structural properties
, except for mere empirical ones, underlying this all-pervasive approach is an assumption that joint relationships or richer structural properties are redundant to achieve optimal throughput over multi-user q-channels. Another aspect is that studies in multi-user q-channels are focused on
2-
user channels. This restriction is possibly motivated by a belief that optimal coding strategies for the latter naturally generalize to yield optimal strategies for three or more user channels, the latter offering no new degrees of freedom.

In this work, we step beyond this conventional approach and demonstrate that multi-user q-channels possess new degrees of freedom that cannot be exploited via unstructured IID codes. In our first step (Sec.
IV
), we put forth a coding strategy based on jointly designed coset codes, wherein two constituent codes possess tightly coupled algebraic closure properties. See Fig.
9
. Crucially, we design a new decoding POVM that can decode bi-variate interference more efficiently than unstructured IID codes. To accomplish this, our new decoding POVM cannot decode into codes that are designed, but ‘functions of codebooks’. As we elaborate in due course, we face a specific challenge of simultaneous decoding not encountered and/or resolved by Sen’s work. Resorting to conditional typical decoding POVMs, designing new tilting maps
[
5
]
, we derive a new inner bound (Thm.
2
). Identifying specific instances (Exs.
1
,
2
), we analytically prove (Prop.
11
,
12
) that our derived inner bound is strictly larger than that achievable via any unstructured IID code based strategy.

Having thus established the need to go beyond unstructured IID codes and incorporate coset codes, we recognize (Rem.
1
) that the latter does not substitute for the former. We therefore design a general coding strategy comprising of two layers (i) an unstructured IID code layer and (ii) a coset code layer with a specific inter-relationships (Fig.
12
) between the codes. Crucially, we combine this with a decoding POVM that can
simultaneously decode
into a
combination of unstructured IID codes and ‘functions of codebooks’
as mentioned earlier. Characterizing its performance we derive a new inner bound (Thm.
2
,
3
) for the
3-
CQIC in the asymptotic regime in Sec.
V
. The latter inner bound subsumes the current known largest based on unstructured IID codes (Rem.
6
), and as demonstrated via Props.
11
,
12
strictly enlarges for specific
3-
CQICs. Thms.
2
,
3
can be naturally generalized to yield analogous inner bounds for bit carrying capacity of
3-
QICs (Rem.
7
). These are our
main contributions
.

The core of our idea lies in the recognition that Rxs over a
3-
CQIC suffer from
bi-variate
interferences owing to the presence of
two
interfering transmissions that each Rx has to contend with. This motivates Rxs operating on a
3-
CQIC to decode
bi-variate
interferences, or more generally bi-variate functions,
efficiently
. As demonstrated in an earlier work
[
14
]
, coding strategies based on unstructured IID codes are inherently incapable of efficiently decoding bi-variate functions. Jointly designed coset codes possessing
inter-
and
intra-
structural properties coupled with new decoding POVMs that exploit these algebraic structures outperform unstructured IID codes, as also evidenced in a series of classical works
[
1
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
.

It is evident from past research
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
2
,
5
,
6
]
that lifting up coding techniques from a classical channel to a general q-channel, analyzing performance and characterizing corresponding inner bounds for the latter is strewn with several challenges. It took over two decades to elevate solution for the capacity of the point-to-point (PTP) pure state CQ channel
[
19
]
to the general PTP CQ channel
[
20
,
21
]
. The problem of designing and analyzing simultaneous decoding is an important case in point. While this is an exercise in a classical information theory graduate course, lifting this up to design and analyze a quantum simultaneous decoder, even in the asymptotic regime, took over fifteen years
[
5
,
6
,
4
]
since Winter’s recognition of the challenge
[
22
, Sec. VIII]
. Evidently, this pursuit has required, and resulted in new ideas of TSA
[
6
,
4
,
5
]
. Another example is that of binning
[
7
]
. The challenge of analyzing binning has resulted in mis-steps
[
2
]
(see
[
13
, Sec. I]
) resulting in Savov and Wilde’s technique of overcounting
[
13
]
. In fact, owing to the latter’s limitations
[
24
]
, this challenge remains unresolved.

Designing and analyzing an efficient coding strategy based on coset codes for communicating over a general
3-
CQIC entails addressing all of the above challenges in a more involved setting with jointly designed coset codes. Firstly, exploiting jointly designed codes requires decoding the bi-variate function efficiently. This entails decoding the function output, while being oblivious to its arguments. To elaborate, the decoding POVM must decode into a new collection of sequences (Rem.
3
), obtained by applying the bi-variate function to all pairs of the original two coset codes, single-letter wise. The decoding POVM must therefore be designed to decode into ‘functions of codebooks’ - an aspect unaddressed in previous works involving unstructured IID codes, including
[
5
,
6
]
. The subspaces into which tilting must be performed, and the tilting maps themselves need to be respecified to enable bi-variate function decoding. Secondly, as mentioned earlier and elaborated in Rem.
1
, we are further required to design and analyze a decoder POVM that simultaneously decodes into a combination of ’functions of coset codes’ and unstructured IID codes. Thirdly, coset code strategies invariably entail binning. While use of unstructured IID codes lets us choose codewords IID from any target distribution, imposing algebraic closure forces codewords to be only
pairwise independent
and
uniformly distributed
. Therefore, we have to employ binning
[
7
]
to sieve out codewords whose empirical distribution matches the target distribution. As elaborated in
[
24
, Sec. 3 prior to Defn. 1]
, the overcounting technique is not amenable in a three receiver scenario. Taking this opportunity, we adopt a likelihood encoder to analyze binning. This enables us to seamlessly separate the analysis of binning and TSA at the decoder (Rem.
4
). In conjunction with these, we identify the necessary analytical tools to combine these different elements in a manner that facilitates an information-theoretic study. Fourthly, characterizing inner bounds corresponding to arbitrary finite fields and generic single-letter distributions is a key aspect of our work. Our study of the non-commuting non-additive
3-
CQIC (Ex.
2
) culminating in Prop.
12
portrays the utility and need for a broader theory involving structured codes possessing algebraic closure properties. Indeed, while the additive structure of Ex.
1
might lead one to conclude rate gains are only for additive scenarios, Prop.
12
puts such thoughts to rest.

Our work builds on considerable prior findings
[
22
,
13
,
23
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
. Holevo
[
20
]
, Schumacher and Westmoreland
[
21
]
laid the first foundation stones by characterizing the capacity of a PTP CQ channel. Subsequent notions of typical subspaces and powerful operator inequalities
[
28
]
set the stage to lift up classical coding techniques to multi-terminal q-channels. Reporting challenges with a quantum simultaneous decoder, Winter
[
22
]
formulated notions of a
gentle
(or
tender

measurement
) to characterize the capacity of a CQ multiple access channel (CQMAC)
[
22
]
. Centrality of a simultaneous decoder for network q-channels, particularly for one-shot studies, the reported challenges spurred much investigation. Fawzi et. al.’s
[
23
]
two Rx simultaneous decoder and Sen’s
[
2
]
sequential decoder contributed alternate approaches. Solving a long-standing challenge by inventing techniques of TSA, Sen’s findings
[
5
,
4
]
have enabled him to prove the achievability of natural CQ analogues of best-known inner bounds for several multi-terminal q-channels
[
6
]
in the general one-shot regime. Note that our work focuses exclusively on an asymptotic study. This is owed to the fact that linear codes of block-length
1
are trivial, and one-shot coset code studies entail a new approach.

We briefly state the significance of our findings. Unstructured IID code based strategies form the defacto coding strategies
[
22
,
13
,
23
,
6
,
25
,
29
]
in network quantum information theory (QIT). In this work, we step beyond this conventional approach and design new strategies based on jointly designed coset codes, derive new inner bounds that subsume all current known unstructured IID code based inner bounds and strictly enlarge upon the same for identified examples. A key challenge is to design and analyze simultaneous decoding of coset and unstructured IID codes. While Sen
[
5
,
6
]
has proposed a new solution to perform simultaneous decoding, his techniques remain to be new, have not yet been employed more broadly, and is restricted to simultaneous decoding of unstructured IID codes. Our contributions must be viewed within this context. We enhance Sen’s technique to performs simultaneous decoding of coset and unstructured IID codes. These findings, in conjunction with our study of
3-
CQBCs
[
30
]
, maybe viewed as building a new framework of coset code based strategies for network QIT.

Organization
: Since we are combining techniques that are either relatively new
[
5
]
, or outside mainstream quantum information theory - such as coset codes - we present our findings in three pedagogical steps to facilitate comprehension. Following preliminaries in Sec.
II
, we elaborate on the main ideas underlying coset codes by designing our coset coding strategies in the context of three carefully chosen examples in Sec.
III
. Building on Rem.
1
, we present our first step that combines all elements in Sec.
IV
. Owing to a fewer number of codes, we are able to detail all elements of our proof in this step. The characterized inner bound therein is also proven to be strictly larger for identified non-commutative additive and non-additive examples. Step II in Sec.
V-A
presents the full suite of coset codes used to tackle all bi-variate interferences and a corresponding inner bound (Thm.
2
). Finally, as needed, we enlarge the inner bound presented in Step II by including IID unstructured codes in Step III to characterize the final desired inner bound in Thm.
3
. Throughout, we have stressed on detailed and clearly justified proofs.

II
Preliminaries and Problem Statement

We adopt standard notation, specifically as in
[
31
]
, supplemented with the following. For
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
[K]\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{1,\ldots,K\right\}
. For prime
\upsilon\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
will denote a finite field of size
\upsilon
with
\oplus
denoting field addition in
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
(i.e., mod
-\upsilon
). For a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}),\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
denote the collection of linear, positive and density operators acting on
\mathcal{H}
respectively.

We let an
underline
denote an appropriate aggregation of objects. For example,
\underline{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{X}_{3}
,
\underline{x}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\underline{\mathcal{X}}
and in regards to Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{i}}:i\in[3]
, we let
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\otimes_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal{H}_{Y_{i}}
. Let
*
denote the binary convolution:
p*q\coloneqq p(1-q)+(1-p)q
. For
p\in[0,1]
,
h_{b}(p)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}-p\log_{2}p-(1-p)\log_{2}(1-p)
denote the binary entropy function. For
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
,
\|A\|_{1}
denotes the trace norm and
\|A\|_{\infty}
denotes the operator norm. A positive operator valued measurement (POVM) is a collection of positive operators that sum to the identity operator. When we say a POVM
\theta_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\theta_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\}
, we assume that
\theta_{a}
is positive for
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\theta_{a}=I
where
\mathcal{A}
is a finite set. We abbreviate probability mass function, conditional typical projector, and unconditional typical projector as PMF, C-Typ-Proj and U-Typ-Proj respectively.

Consider a (generic)
3-
CQIC

(\rho_{\underline{x}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{Y}}):\underline{x}\in\underline{\mathcal{X}},\kappa_{j}:j\in[3])
specified through (i) three finite sets
\mathcal{X}_{j}:j\in[3]
, (ii) three Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}:j\in[3]
, (iii) a collection
(\rho_{\underline{x}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{Y}}):\underline{x}\in\underline{\mathcal{X}})
of density operators and (iv) three non-negative valued cost functions
\kappa_{j}:\mathcal{X}_{j}\rightarrow[0,\infty):j\in[3]
. The cost function is assumed to be additive, i.e., cost expended by Tx
j
in preparing the state
\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{1t}x_{2t}x_{3t}}
is
\overline{\kappa}_{j}^{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\kappa_{j}(x_{jt})
. Reliable communication on a
3-
CQIC entails identifying a code.

Definition 1
.

A
3-
CQIC code
c=(n,\underline{\mathcal{M}},\underline{e},\uline{\mu})
of block-length
n
consists of three (i) message index sets
\mathcal{M}_{j}:j\in[3]
, (ii) encoder maps
e_{j}:\mathcal{M}_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{j}^{n}:j\in[3]
and (iii) POVMs
\mu_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\mu_{j,m_{j}}:\mathcal{H}_{j}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{j}^{\otimes n}:m_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{j}\}:j\in[3]
. The average error probability of the
3-
CQIC code
(n,\underline{\mathcal{M}},\underline{e},\underline{\mu})
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\underline{e},\underline{\mu})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}1-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}||\mathcal{M}_{2}||\mathcal{M}_{3}|}\sum_{\underline{m}\in\underline{\mathcal{M}}}\tr\left\{\left(\mu_{1,m_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2,m_{2}}\otimes\mu_{3,m_{3}}\right)\left(\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{1t}(m_{1})x_{1t}(m_{2})x_{1t}(m_{3})}\right)\right\}.

where
\underline{\mu}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\otimes_{j=1}^{3}\mu_{j}
and
(x_{jt}(m_{j}):1\leq t\leq n)=x_{j}^{n}(m_{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}e_{j}(m_{j})
for
j\in[3]
. The average cost per symbol of transmitting message
\underline{m}\in\underline{\mathcal{M}}
is

\uline{\tau}(\uline{e}|\underline{m})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\overline{\kappa}_{j}^{n}(e_{j}(m_{j})):j\in[3]\right)
, and the average cost per symbol of
3-
CQIC code is
\underline{\tau}(\underline{e})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{|\underline{\mathcal{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}\in\underline{\mathcal{M}}}\underline{\tau}(\underline{e}|\underline{m})
.

Definition 2
.

A rate-cost vector
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\in[0,\infty)^{6}
is achievable if there exists a sequence of
3-
CQIC code
(n,\underline{\mathcal{M}}^{(n)},\underline{e}^{(n)},\uline{\mu}^{(n)})
for which
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}(\underline{e}^{(n)},\uline{\mu}^{(n)})=0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log|\mathcal{M}_{j}^{(n)}|=R_{j},\mbox{ and }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\underline{\tau}(\underline{e})_{j}\leq\tau_{j}:j\in[3].

The capacity-cost region
\mathscr{C}
of the
3-
CQIC is the set of all achievable rate-cost vectors and
\mathscr{C}(\underline{\tau})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\underline{R}:(\underline{R},\underline{\tau})\in\mathscr{C}\}
.

The novelty of our proposed coding strategy is based on
coset codes
- an ensemble of codes possessing algebraic closure property. Specifically, we employ
nested coset codes
(NCC) which we define below.

Definition 3
.

An
(n,k,g,b^{n})
coset code built over a finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
is specified via (i) a generator matrix
g\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k\times n}
and (ii) a bias
b^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}
. We let
u^{n}(a)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}ag\oplus b^{n}
for
a\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k}
denote the codewords of the coset code
(n,k,g,b^{n})
, where as mentioned above,
\oplus
denotes field addition in
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
. The rate of this code is
\frac{k}{n}\log(\Prime)
.

Definition 4
.

An
(n,k,l,g_{I},g_{O/I},b^{n})
NCC over
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
consists of (i) generator matrices
g_{I}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k\times n}
and
g_{O/I}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l\times n}
, and
(ii) bias vector
b^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}
. We let
u^{n}(a,m)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}ag_{I}\oplus mg_{O/I}\oplus b^{n}
, for
(a,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k}\times\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l}
denote codewords of the coset code. The collection
\left(u^{n}(a,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}:a\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k}\right)
is referred to as the coset corresponding to message
m\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l}
.

III
Strict suboptimality of unstructured IID codes for non-commutative
3-
CQICs

Our goal here is to
explain
through a set of examples
how and why
coset codes can yield strictly higher rates over
3-
CQICs.
Our first non-commutative example - Ex.
1
- is an ’additive’ one and illustrates how coset codes can enable optimal decoding of a bi-variate additive interference. Our second non-commutative Ex.
2
establishes that higher throughput can be obtained even for non-‘additive’
3-
CQICs. In addition to portraying rate gains for ‘non-additive’
3-
CQICs, Ex.
2
serves to guide (Rem.
1
) the structure of a general inner bound using coset codes. For pedagogical reasons we begin with a simple commutative Ex.
0
.

.

Notation 1
.

For
x\in\{0,1\}
,
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, define
\sigma_{\delta}(x)=(1-\delta)\outerproduct{1-x}{1-x}+\delta\outerproduct{x}{x}
. For
\varphi\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, define
\gamma(x)=\outerproduct{0}{0}\mathds{1}{\{x=0\}}+\outerproduct{v_{\varphi}}{v_{\varphi}}\mathds{1}{\{x=1\}}
where
\ket{v_{\varphi}}=\left[\cos(\varphi)~\sin(\varphi)\right]^{t}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{j}(\uline{\tau})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\max\{R_{j}:(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\in\mathscr{C}(\uline{\tau})\}
and
C_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{C}_{j}(\infty)
denote the interference-free capacity of user
j
with and without input-cost constraints, respectively, for
j\in[3]
.

Through each of the Examples
0
,
1
and
2
, we investigate the achievability of the rate triple
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
under the condition

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{3}>C_{1}.

(1)

Example 0
.

Consider a
3-
CQIC with binary inputs and qubit output spaces, i.e.,
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
for
j\in[3]
. The channel is defined as
\rho_{\underline{x}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sigma_{\delta_{1}}(x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus x_{3})\otimes\sigma_{\delta_{2}}(x_{2})\otimes\sigma_{\delta_{3}}(x_{3})
for
\underline{x}\in\uline{\mathcal{X}}
.
Inputs of users 2 and 3 are not constrained, i.e.,
\kappa_{j}(0)=\kappa_{j}(1)=0
for
j=2,3
. User 1’s input is constrained with respect to a Hamming cost function, i.e.,
\kappa_{1}(x)=x:x\in\{0,1\}
, to an average cost of
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
per symbol.

For simplicity, let
\delta_{2}=\delta_{3}=\delta
. Since
\rho_{\underline{x}}
for
\underline{x}\in\mathcal{\underline{X}}
are commuting, Ex.
0
can be identified via a 3-user classical binary IC (Fig.
3
) with
Y_{1}=X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\oplus N_{1},Y_{k}=X_{k}\oplus N_{k}
where
N_{1}\sim
Ber
(\delta_{1})
,
N_{k}\sim
Ber
(\delta)
for
k=2,3
. Input
X_{1}
is constrained to a Hamming cost
\tau_{1}
, and
X_{2},X_{3}
are unconstrained. We will argue, as in that
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
, under the condition (
1
), is not achievable using unstructured IID codes, but is achievable using coset codes as long as
C_{1}>\mathcal{C}_{1}+\max\{\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\}
(note that here
\mathcal{C}_{2}=\mathcal{C}_{3}
). Despite being similar to that in
[
17
]
, we provide this argument here to facilitate pedagogy.

Let’s fix user
k
’s rate
R_{k}=\mathcal{C}_{k}
for
k=2,3
. This forces
X_{2},X_{3}
to be independent Ber
(\frac{1}{2})
RVs, implying
I(X_{1};Y_{1})=0
. This in turn implies that Rx
1

has to
decode interference, fully or partially, to achieve
any
positive rate. How do unstructured IID codes enable Rx
1
to decode interference?

The unstructured IID coding strategy entails that Tx
k\in\{2,3\}
splits its information into
U_{k}
and
X_{k}
, Rx
1
decodes
U_{2},U_{3},X_{1}
, while Rx
k\in\{2,3\}
decodes
U_{k},X_{k}
.
Suppose
p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}X_{2}}p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
is the corresponding single-letter test channel induced by such a coding strategy.

For such a test channel, so long as
H(X_{k}|U_{k})>0
for either
k\in\{2,3\}
, it can be shown
[
17
]
that
H(X_{2}\oplus X_{3}|U_{2},U_{3})>0
, implying that Rx 1 cannot achieve
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Indeed, note that the latter is the interference-free, cost-constrained capacity of Rx 1, which cannot be achieved if Rx 1 ignores the full interference
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}
. If (
1
) holds, it can be shown that there exists
no
PMF
p_{X_{1}}p_{X_{2}U_{2}}p_{X_{3}U_{3}}
for which
p_{X_{1}X_{2}}=\mbox{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})\otimes\mbox{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})

and

H(X_{2}|U_{2})=H(X_{3}|U_{3})=0
. Thus, (
1
) precludes unstructured IID codes from achieving
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
. How can linear codes help under condition (
1
)?

Observe that users
2
and
3
’s channels are simple binary symmetric channels with crossover probability
\delta
(BSC(
\delta
)). Let
\lambda
be a linear code of rate
1-h_{b}(\delta)
that achieves capacity of BSC
(\delta)
. By employing
cosets
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
of the same linear code

\lambda
for both users
2
and
3
, the collection of all interference patterns - all possible sums of user
2
and
3
’s codewords - is contained in
another coset
\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3}
of the same linear code
. Crucially,
\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3}
is of the same rate
1-h_{b}(\delta)
. Rx
1
just decodes into
\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3}
and hunts for the interference
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}
. It can recover the
sum of the codewords
even while being oblivious to the
pair
, so long as
\mathcal{C}_{1}+\max\{\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\}=\mathcal{C}_{1}+1-h_{b}(\delta)<C_{1}
holds.

Now, suppose
\delta_{2}\neq\delta_{3}
, we would have chosen the smaller code as the sub-coset of the larger code, thereby containing all possible mod
-2
sums of the codewords within a coset of the larger code of rate
\max\{\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\}
. Then, (
1
) does
not preclude
linear codes from achieving
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
. In summary, we conclude the following, whose formal proof can be found in
[
17
]
.

Proposition 1
.

Consider Ex.
0
with

\tau,\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3}
satisfying (
1
).
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is not achievable using any known unstructured IID coding strategy but is achievable using a coset codes strategy, as long as
C_{1}>\mathcal{C}_{1}+\max\{\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\}
.

Example 1
.

Consider a
3-
CQIC with binary input sets and qubit output spaces, that is
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
for
j\in[3]
. The channel is defined as
\rho_{\underline{x}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\gamma(x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus x_{3})\otimes\sigma_{\delta_{2}}(x_{2})\otimes\sigma_{\delta_{3}}(x_{3})
, for
\underline{x}\in\uline{\mathcal{X}}
.
Inputs of users 2 and 3 are not constrained, i.e.,
\kappa_{j}(0)=\kappa_{j}(1)=0
for
j=2,3
. User 1’s input is constrained with respect to a Hamming cost function, i.e.,
\kappa_{1}(x)=x:x\in\{0,1\}
, to an average cost of
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
per symbol.

Ex.
1
is a non-commutative one. Indeed, the states
\gamma(0),\gamma(1)
do not share a common diagonalization basis. Let us first bound the above capabilities of an unstructured IID code based strategy.

Proposition 2
.

Consider the
3-
CQIC in Ex.
1
under condition (
1
).
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is not achievable using any known unstructured coding strategy.

Proof.

A similar train of arguments as used for Ex.
0
will enable us to prove this statement. See Appendix
B-A
for a proof.
∎

Our prior discussion in regards to Ex.
0
should indicate to the reader that we could obtain rate gains if Tx
2
and
3
employ coset codes corresponding to the uniform distribution and Rx
1
employs a POVM that enables it to decode the sum of the codewords chosen by Tx
2
and
3
. We refer the reader to Prop.
11
where it is formally stated and proven that
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is achievable using a coset code strategy. Prop.
11
in conjunction with Prop.
2
therefore establishes that coset code strategies can strictly outperform unstructured IID codes over non-commutative
3-
CQICs. We now go one more step.

Observe that establishing the above rate gains required our coset code strategy to achieve rates corresponding to uniform distribution induced on finite fields. This requirement aligns well with conventional information-theoretic proof techniques since a randomly chosen coset code (or linear code) induces uniform distribution on the underlying finite field and hence is capable of achieving rates corresponding to the uniform distribution. A general coset code based strategy must, at the very least, enable a Rx to decode the sum of codewords from coset codes corresponding to the uniform distribution. Does it suffice to achieve rates corresponding to the uniform distribution on the finite fields? The following example, in conjunction with the ensuing discussion demonstrates achieving rates corresponding to non-uniform distributions induced over finite fields and designing POVMs to decode the sum of chosen codewords can enable us to achieve rate gains over a much larger class of
3-
CQICs specifically even over ‘non-additive’
3-
CQICs.

Example 2
.

Consider a
3-
CQIC with binary inputs and qubit output spaces, i.e.,
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
for
j\in[3]
. The channel is defined as
\rho_{\underline{x}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\gamma\left(x_{1}\oplus(x_{2}\lor x_{3})\right)\otimes\sigma_{\delta_{2}}(x_{2})\otimes\sigma_{\delta_{3}}(x_{3})
for
\underline{x}\in\uline{\mathcal{X}}
, where
\lor
denotes the logical OR. User j’s input is constrained with respect to a Hamming cost function, i.e.,
\kappa_{j}(x)=x:x\in\{0,1\}
, to an average cost of
\tau_{j}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
for
j\in[3]
per symbol.

In addition to being non-commutative, the above
3-
CQIC is also ‘non-additive’. Can coset code strategies yield higher rates? Let us once again begin by bounding on the above capabilities of any unstructured coding based strategy.

Proposition 3
.

Consider the
3-
CQIC in Ex.
2

under condition (
1
).
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is not achievable using any known unstructured coding strategy.

Proof.

See Appendix
B-B
for a proof.
∎

Can coset codes achieve this rate triple
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
? Instead of viewing the input alphabets of users
2
and
3
to be binary, suppose we (i) consider
p_{X_{2}}(0)=p_{X_{3}}(0)=p_{X_{2}}(1)=p_{X_{3}}(1)=\frac{1}{2}
and
p_{X_{2}}(2)=p_{X_{3}}(2)=0
with input alphabets
\mathcal{X}_{2}=\mathcal{X}_{3}=\{0,1,2\}
of users
2
and
3
‘viewed’ as the ternary field, (ii) build coset codes over the ternary field for users
2
and
3
corresponding to the above non-uniform distribution while ensuring no codewords possess the symbol
2
, and enable (iii) Rx
1
to decode the ternary field sum of the chosen ‘ternary’ codewords. Then note that, since
H(X_{2}\lor X_{3}|X_{2}\oplus_{3}X_{3})=0
(see Fig.
3
), Rx 1 can recover the full interference. Indeed, the recovered ternary addition of the chosen codewords can be mapped to the interference pattern - the logical OR of the chosen codewords. Since
p_{X_{2}}(2)=p_{X_{3}}(2)=0
, if the codewords of the code induce this chosen non-uniform PMF they should be able to input the same on the
3-
CQICs. We are led to the following.

Remark 1
.

As is evident from the above discussion, reinforced via a formal proof provided in Prop.
12
. As we will see in Sec.
IV
, one can achieve
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
for Ex.
2
if we can build coset codes over arbitrary finite fields and achieve rates corresponding to
non-uniform distribution
. Next, we have to be able to decode bi-variate interference at all three Rxs, requiring each Tx to encode parts of their message using coset codes. Thm.
2
presents such a coding strategy and a corresponding inner bound, wherein each Rx decodes bi-variate interference and the induced single-letter distribution is arbitrary. This does not suffice since each Rx over a
3-
CQIC encounters a combination of uni-variate and bi-variate interferences. The former is more efficiently decoded via unstructured IID codes and the latter via coset codes. We will therefore have to design a two-layer coding strategy that combines both unstructured IID codes and coset codes to manage uni-variate and bi-variate interference efficiently. The need for this two-layer coding strategy is also evident from an earlier related work of Ahlswede and Han
[
32
, Sec. VI]
in the context of a source coding problem, which has been built upon in
[
17
, Thm. 4]
.

Building on the above remark, our goal through the rest of the article is to design and analyze a two-layer coding strategy comprising both coset and unstructured IID codes. While the former enables efficient decoding of bi-variate interferences, the latter assists in decoding uni-variate interference components efficiently. This general two-layer coding strategy is developed in three pedagogical steps. Our first step, described in Sec.
IV
, will be to design and analyze a coding strategy that manages only bi-variate interference suffered by a single Rx. Specifically, only Rx 1 will decode bi-variate interference, which is facilitated by Tx 2 and 3 encoding their messages using coset codes (see Fig.
4
).

IV
A Coset Codes Strategy to Manage bi-variate Interference at a Single Receiver

The goal of the first step presented here is to provide a proof in a simplified scenario that
combines
all the new elements - simultaneous decoding of unstructured and coset codes using TSA, and its combination with a likelihood encoder. The derived inner bound permits a compact description obtained via Fourier-Motzkin elimination as stated in Thm.
1
. Moreover, it enables us to prove that coset codes are strictly more efficient for additive and non-additive, non-commutative Ex.
1
and
2
respectively.

The coding strategy we present in this step employs two codes - a nested coset code and an IID unstructured code - for users
2
and
3
respectively, and a single unstructured IID code for user
1
. See Fig.
4
,
6
wherein Rvs
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
are encoded using coset codes built on the same finite field and
X_{k}
for
k\in[3]
is encoded via unstructured IID codes. Rx
1
has to simultaneously decode
U_{2}\oplus U_{3}
and
X_{1}
, while Rxs
j
simultaneously decode
U_{j},X_{j}
for
j\in\{2,3\}
. An informed reader will recognize that the new nontrivial aspect of a proof revolves only around the design and analysis of Rx
1
’s simultaneous decoder. Indeed, (i) the latter involves decoding the sum of the codewords chosen by Rxs
2
and
3
- a new element, and (ii) simultaneous decoding of Rxs
2
and
3
can be analyzed in a standard fashion using the techniques of Fawzi et. al
[
23
, Sec. V.B]
which circumvents the need for Sen’s TSA
[
5
]
. To facilitate easy comprehension of the proof of Thm.
1
and, moreover, highlight combination of TSA with decoding the sum of codewords, we prove a simplified coding strategy in Lemma
1
wherein Rxs
2
and
3
do not split their message, i.e.
X_{j}=f_{j}(U_{j})
for
j\in\{2,3\}
. Proof of Thm.
1
can be obtained by just combining the proof of Lemma
1
with the simultaneous decoder of Fawzi et. al
[
23
, Sec. V.B]
and its analysis. Proof of Lemma
1
involves less clutter owing to a reduced number of Rvs even while capturing the non-trivial Rx
1
’s simultaneous decoder and its analysis.

Fig. 4:
Depiction of Rvs of the coding strategy.

Lemma 1
.

A rate-cost triple
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})
is achievable if there exists (i) a finite field
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{3}=\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
, (ii) PMFs
p_{X_{1}}
,
p_{U_{2}}
,
p_{U_{3}}
on
\mathcal{X}_{1}
,
\mathcal{U}_{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{3}
respectively, and (iii) functions
f_{j}:\mathcal{U}_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{j}
for
j=2,3
, such that
\mathbb{E}[\kappa(X_{1})]\leq\tau_{1}
,
\sum_{u_{j}}p_{U_{j}}(u_{j})\kappa(f_{j}(u_{j}))\leq\tau_{j}
,

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(X_{1};Y_{1},U_{2}\oplus U_{3}),

\displaystyle R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{j};Y_{j}),

\displaystyle R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2}\oplus U_{3};Y_{1},X_{1})-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3})+H(U_{j}),\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle R_{1}+R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2}\oplus U_{3},X_{1};Y_{1})-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3})+H(U_{j}),\mbox{ for }\>j=2,3\>\>\mbox{holds},

with all information quantities being computed with respect to the state

\displaystyle\rho^{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}U_{2}U_{3}X_{1}\underline{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{u,u_{2},u_{3},x_{1}}p_{X_{1}}(x_{1})p_{U_{2}}(u_{2})p_{U_{3}}(u_{3})\mathds{1}{\{u=u_{2}\oplus u_{3}\}}\outerproduct{u~u_2~u_3~x_1}{u~u_2~u_3~x_1}\otimes\rho^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}_{x_{1},f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{3}(u_{3})}.

The above inner bound can be strictly larger for non-commutative
3-
CQICs. For a quick perusal of this fact the reader is referred to Sec.
IV-A
. We now proceed to a proof of the above lemma.

Proof.

As stated in the hypothesis, choose a generic finite field and let
\upsilon
denote its cardinality. Set
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{3}=\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
be this finite field of cardinality
\upsilon
. Choose generic functions
f_{j}:\mathcal{U}_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{j}
, for
j=2,3
and generic PMFs
p_{X_{1}},p_{U_{2}}
and
p_{U_{3}}
on
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{3}
respectively, that satisfy the cost constraints
\mathbb{E}[\kappa(X_{1})]\leq\tau_{1}
,
\sum_{u_{j}}p_{U_{j}}(u_{j})\kappa(f_{j}(u_{j}))\leq\tau_{j}
and set PMF
p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{X}}=p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}}p_{U_{3}}\mathds{1}{\{X_{2}=f_{2}(U_{2})\}}\mathds{1}{\{X_{3}=f_{3}(U_{3})\}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{2}\times\mathcal{U}_{3}\times\underline{\mathcal{X}})
. Having chosen these objects, fix the same throughout the rest of the discussion and let the joint quantum state be defined in (
1
) with respect to this PMF
p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{X}}
. Let
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\in[0,\infty)^{3}
be a rate triple satisfying all the bounds stated in the hypothesis of the lemma statement with respect to this joint single-letter quantum state in (
1
). For every rate triple
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
satisfying the above bounds, there exist auxiliary parameters
B_{2}>0,B_{3}>0
such that
R_{1}>0,R_{j}>0
for
j=2,3
and

\displaystyle B_{j}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\log(\Prime)-H(U_{j}),

\displaystyle B_{j}+R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{j};Y_{j})+\log(\Prime)-H(U_{j}),

\displaystyle R_{1}+B_{j}+R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(X_{1},U_{2}\oplus U_{3};Y_{1})+\log(\Prime)-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3}),

\displaystyle B_{j}+R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2}\oplus U_{3};Y_{1}|X_{1})+\log(\Prime)-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3}),\mbox{ for }j=2,3,\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(X_{1};Y_{1}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}).

This existence can be verified by a standard Fourier Motzkin elimination (FME)
[
33
, Appendix D]
technique. Specifically, by eliminating
B_{2},B_{3}
by the FME technique we obtain the bounds stated in the lemma statement. Having identified the parameters that define our coding strategy, we now describe the code structure.

Fig. 5:
The coding strategy involves three codes, depicted in the grey box - two of them being NCCs built on the finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
and an unstructured IID code built on
\mathcal{X}_{1}
. Rx
1
, in addition to decoding into its code, decodes into the collection of ‘codewords’ in the orange dotted box. Specifically, the collection of the codewords in the orange dotted box is obtained by adding all pairs of codewords in the
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
codes.

Code structure
: The coding strategy comprises three codes - one for each user. See Fig.
5
. User
1
employs an unstructured code
c_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}):m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\}
. User
2
and
3
employ NCCs built over the same finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
. More importantly, as we will elaborate below the two NCCs are chosen such that the smaller of the two is a subcoset of the larger. For
j=2,3
, set

\displaystyle\mbox{{ $k_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\lceil\frac{nB_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\rceil$} and $l_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\lfloor\frac{nR_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\rfloor$ and define }\mathsf{b},\mathsf{s}\mbox{ such that }\{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{s}\}=\{2,3\}\mbox{ and }k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}\geq k_{\mathsf{s}}+l_{\mathsf{s}}.

(2)

For
j=2,3
, user
j
’s code is specified through (i) a NCC
(n,k_{j},l_{j},g_{I}^{j},g_{O|I}^{j},b_{j}^{n})
built over the finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
with parameters
g_{I}^{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{j}\times n}
,
g_{O|I}^{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l_{j}\times n}
, bias
b_{j}^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}
,
k_{j}:=\lceil\frac{nB_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\rceil
and
l_{j}:=\lfloor\frac{nR_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\rfloor
and (ii) a binning map
a_{j}:\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l_{j}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{j}}
. Let
g_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}g_{I}^{j}\\
g_{O/I}^{j}\end{array}\!\!\right]
for
j=2,3
. These generator matrices are identified such that
g_{\mathsf{b}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}g_{\mathsf{s}}\\
g_{\Delta}\end{array}\!\!\right]
. This choice of the generator matrices ensures that the smaller of these NCCs is a subcoset of the larger NCC. This will also be imposed by the distribution of the corresponding random codebooks with respect to which we analyze the average error probability. For
j=2,3
, let
u^{n}_{j}(a_{j}(m_{j}),m_{j})
denote the chosen codeword from the coset
c_{j}(m_{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{u^{n}_{j}(a_{j}(m_{j}),m_{j}):a_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{j}}\}
corresponding to message
m_{j}
.

Remark 2
.

We highlight the intra- and inter-structural properties of the codes. Firstly,
c_{2},c_{3}
, being coset code, possess certain algebraic closure properties bereft in unstructured code strategies. Secondly, the rows of
g_{\mathsf{s}}
being contained in the rows of
g_{\mathsf{b}}
implies that by adding all codewords of user
2
’s NCC to all codewords of user
3
’s NCC, we obtain a collection of vectors of cardinality at most
\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}
and not
\upsilon^{k_{1}+l_{1}+k_{2}+l_{2}}
. We crucially exploit this sum containment in the decoding specified below.

Encoding
: Upon observing
m_{1}
the Tx
1
sends the codeword
x^{n}_{1}(m_{1})
. For
j=2,3
, upon observing
m_{j}
the Tx
j
sends
f_{j}^{n}(u_{j}^{n}(a_{j}(m_{j}),m_{j}))
. Here, note that
f_{j}(\cdot)
is evaluated letter-by-letter.

Decoding POVM
: We first describe the decoding POVM for Rx 1. Rx 1 decodes the message
m_{1}
and the
sum of the chosen
U_{2}-,U_{3}-
codewords
simultaneously.
Naturally,
m_{1}
is deciphered by decoding into
c_{1}
. In regards to the sum of the chosen
U_{2}-,U_{3}-
codewords, the decoder identifies the ‘sum coset code’
c
with parameters
(n,k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}},g_{\mathsf{b}},b^{n})
, where
b^{n}=b_{2}^{n}\oplus b_{3}^{n}
.
For
s\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}
, we define
u^{n}(s)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}sg_{\mathsf{b}}\oplus b^{n}
to represent a generic codeword in this ‘sum coset code’.
1
1

1

Strictly speaking, one must refer to codewords of this sum coset code as vectors since none of the Txs choose any codeword in this sum coset code explicitly.
If one were to think classically for a moment, Rx
1
having received
y_{1}^{n}
looks for a pair
(x^{n}(m_{1}),u^{n}(s))\in c_{1}\times c
that is jointly typical with the received
y_{1}^{n}
with respect to the joint PMF
p_{X_{1},U_{2}\oplus U_{3},Y_{1}}
. In the following, we construct the simultaneous decoding POVM of Rx
1
to accomplish this in our CQ setting.

Remark 3
.

We direct a reader’s attention to the key differences from conventional unstructured IID code strategies that are evident from the above description. Firstly, the joint algebraic closure relationship induced between
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
by choosing one of them to be a sub-coset of the other. Secondly, the Rx
1
constructs a new collection
c
of codewords into which it decodes. This collection is used by neither Txs. Furthermore, we have to combine these elements with the need to alter the POVMs via TSA to accomplish simultaneous decoding.

If simultaneous decoding required no newer elements, we would just be constructing conditional typical projectors corresponding to state
\rho_{x_{1},u}^{Y_{1}}
in (
3
). However, as Sen has discovered, simultaneous decoding will require identifying auxiliary spaces and tilting the projectors. We therefore now employ Sen’s TSA technique
[
5
]
to appropriately tilt our identified decoding POVM as described below. We should note here that TSA is performed to handle unions and intersections of typical subspaces, which can overlap in complex ways. Tilting maps rotate such intersecting subspaces in orthogonal directions, thereby increasing their separation. Specifically, for three possible error events corresponding to distinct subspaces, we define two tilting maps for the first two subspaces, representing the case where a single message is decoded incorrectly, while the third subspace, corresponding to errors in both messages, remains untilted. To enable this construction, we first enlarge the original Hilbert space by adding extra dimensions to enable the tilts. We then augment the space to ensure the smoothing property
[
5
]
.

Consider two auxiliary finite sets
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
along with corresponding Hilbert space
{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
and
{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
of dimension
|\mathcal{D}_{1}|
and
|\mathcal{D}_{2}|
respectively. We define the extended space
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}
as follows

\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}\right)^{\otimes n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}\oplus\left(\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\otimes n}\right)\oplus\left(\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\otimes n}\right)

where
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}=\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
In the sequel, we let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}}=\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{\otimes n}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}=\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}
,

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}}=\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}\right)^{\otimes n}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}_{j}}=\mathcal{D}_{j}^{\otimes n}:j\in[2]
. The
G
in the superscript here and throughout in the following denotes the Gelfand-Naimark extension. For
j\in[2]
and
d_{j}^{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{j}^{n}
we define the tilting map
\mathcal{T}_{d_{j}^{n},\eta}:\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}\oplus\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}_{j}}\right)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{d_{j}^{n},\eta}(\ket{h})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\eta^{2}}}\left(\ket{h}+\eta\ket{h}\otimes\ket{d_{j}^{n}}\right),

where
\eta
will be chosen appropriately in the sequel.
Let
\Pi_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}},\Pi_{x_{1}^{n}}^{Y_{1}}
and
\Pi_{u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}
be the C-Typ-Proj with respect to the states
\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1},u}\right)^{\otimes n}
,
\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1}^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1}}\right)^{\otimes n}
and
\rho^{Y_{1}}_{u^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\rho^{Y_{1}}_{u}\right)^{\otimes n}
respectively, and let
\Pi^{Y_{1}}
be the U-Typ-Proj with respect to the state
\left(\rho^{Y_{1}}\right)^{\otimes n}
, where

\displaystyle\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1},u}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u_{2},u_{3}}p_{U_{2}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}(u_{2}|u)p_{U_{3}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}U_{2}}(u_{3}|u,u_{2})\rho_{x_{1},f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{3}(u_{3})}^{Y_{1}},

(3)

\displaystyle\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u_{2},u_{3}}p_{U_{2}}(u_{2})p_{U_{3}}(u_{3})\rho_{x_{1},f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{3}(u_{3})}^{Y_{1}},

(4)

\displaystyle\rho^{Y_{1}}_{u}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

(5)

\displaystyle\rho^{Y_{1}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

(6)

Consider the following POVM elements

\displaystyle G^{1}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}=\Pi_{u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\>\Pi^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\>\Pi_{u^{n}}^{Y_{1}},\>\>G^{2}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}=\Pi_{x_{1}^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\>\Pi_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\>\Pi_{x_{1}^{n}}^{Y_{1}},\mbox{ and }G^{3}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}=\Pi^{Y_{1}}\>\Pi_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\>\Pi^{Y_{1}},

(7)

By Gelfand–Naimark’s Thm.
[

, there exists orthogonal projectors
\overline{G}^{\mathcal{J}}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\in\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}})

that

gives on states in

for

.
Let

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{\mathcal{J}}=I_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}}-\overline{G}^{\mathcal{J}}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}},\mbox{ for }{\mathcal{J}\in\{1,2,3\}}

(8)

be the complement projector. Now, consider

\displaystyle\beta_{x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n},u^{n},d_{2}^{n}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\mathcal{T}_{d^{n}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}},\eta}\left(\overline{B}^{\mathcal{J}}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\right),\mbox{ for }\mathcal{J}\in\{1,2\},\mbox{ and }\beta^{3}_{x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n},u^{n},d_{2}^{n}}=\overline{B}^{3}_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}

(9)

for

represent the untilted projector.
Next, we define
\beta^{*}_{x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n},u^{n},d_{2}^{n}}
as the projector in

whose support is the union of the supports of
\beta^{\mathcal{J}}_{x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n},u^{n},d_{2}^{n}}
for all
\mathcal{J}\in\{1,2,3\}
. Let
\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}}
be the orthogonal projector in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}}
onto
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}
. We define the square root measurement
[
31
,
34
]

\{\mu_{m_{1},s}^{Y_{1}}:(m_{1},s)\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\times\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}\}
as

\displaystyle\mu_{m_{1},s}^{Y_{1}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\widehat{m}_{1},\widehat{s}}\gamma^{*}_{(x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n})(\widehat{m}_{1}),(u^{n},d_{2}^{n})(\widehat{s})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gamma^{*}_{(x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n})(m_{1}),(u^{n},d_{2}^{n})(s)}\left(\sum_{\widehat{m}_{1},\widehat{s}}\gamma^{*}_{(x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n})(\widehat{m}_{1}),(u^{n},d_{2}^{n})(\widehat{s})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}},\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle\mu^{Y_{1}}_{-1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle I-\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{s}\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1}},\mbox{ where }

\displaystyle\gamma^{*}_{(x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n})(m_{1}),(u^{n},d_{2}^{n})(s)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}
