(10)

Decoding POVM for Rxs
2
and
3
: The decoding POVM for Rxs
2
and
3
are identical. Therefore, we only describe one of them. Rx 2 is only interested in message
m_{2}
. Consequently, to decode
m_{2}
, we use the NCC
(n,k_{2},l_{2},g_{I}^{2},g_{O|I}^{2},b_{2}^{n})
used by Tx 2.
Let
\Pi_{u_{2}^{n}}^{Y_{2}}
and
\Pi^{Y_{2}}
be the typical projectors with respect to the states
\rho_{u_{2}^{n}}^{Y_{2}}=\left(\rho_{u_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\right)^{\otimes n}
and
\left(\rho^{Y_{2}}\right)^{\otimes n}
respectively, where

\displaystyle\rho_{u_{2}}^{Y_{2}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u_{3},x_{1}}p_{U_{3}}(u_{3})p_{X_{1}}(x_{1})\rho_{x_{1},f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{3}(u_{3})}^{Y_{2}},\mbox{ and}

(11)

\displaystyle\rho^{Y_{2}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u_{2},u_{3},x_{1}}p_{U_{2}}(u_{2})p_{U_{3}}(u_{3})p_{X_{1}}(x_{1})\rho_{x_{1},f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{3}(u_{3})}^{Y_{2}}.

(12)

We define the square root measurement
[
31
,
34
]

\{\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}:m_{2}\in[2^{nR_{2}}]\}
as

\displaystyle\hskip-18.06749pt\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\sum_{\widehat{m}_{2},\widehat{a}_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(\widehat{a}_{2},\widehat{m}_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{a_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\right)\left(\sum_{\widehat{m}_{2},\widehat{a}_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(\widehat{a}_{2},\widehat{m}_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!\!\!,\mbox{ and }\mu^{Y_{2}}_{-1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}I-\sum_{m_{2}}\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}.

Error analysis
:
As is standard, we derive an upper bound on the error probability of a good code by averaging the error probability over an ensemble of codes. We begin by specifying the codebook distribution, i.e. the random code distribution, with respect to which this averaging is performed. Towards that end, recall that
g_{j}=\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}g_{I}^{j}\\
g_{O/I}^{j}\end{array}\!\!\right]
for
j=2,3
and these generator matrices satisfy
g_{\mathsf{b}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}g_{\mathsf{s}}\\
g_{\Delta}\end{array}\!\!\right]
, where
\mathsf{b}
is defined as in (
2
). From the code structure, encoding and decoding it is evident that our coding strategy is completely specified via the following objects :
c_{1}=(x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}):m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]),g_{\mathsf{b}},b_{j}^{n}
for
j\in\{2,3\}
,
(d_{1}^{n}(m_{1}):m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}])),(d_{2}^{n}(s):s\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}))
and finally maps
a_{j}:\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l_{j}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{j}}
for
j\in\{2,3\}
. It suffices therefore to specify the joint distribution of these objects. For any choice of the arguments in their respective range spaces, let

\displaystyle P\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\left(X_{1}^{n}(m_{1})=x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}):m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\right),G_{b}=g_{b},\left(B_{j}^{n}=b_{j}^{n}:j=2,3\right),\left(D_{1}^{n}(m_{1})=d_{1}^{n}(m_{1}):m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\right),\\
\left(D_{2}^{n}(s)=d_{2}^{n}(s):s\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}\right),\left(A_{j}(m_{j})=a_{j}(m_{j}):m_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l_{j}}:j=2,3\right)\end{array}\right)

(16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\prod_{m_{1}=1}^{2^{nR_{1}}}p^{n}_{X_{1}}(x^{n}_{1}(m_{1}))\right]\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{2n}}\left[\prod_{m_{1}=1}^{2^{nR_{1}}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{1}|^{n}}\right]\left[\prod_{s=1}^{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{2}|^{n}}\right]\left[\prod_{j=2}^{3}\prod_{m_{j}=1}^{2^{nR_{j}}}\frac{p^{n}_{U_{j}}({a_{j}(m_{j})g_{I}^{j}\oplus m_{j}g_{O/I}^{j}\oplus b_{j}^{n}})}{\sum_{\alpha_{j}\in\upsilon^{k_{j}}}p^{n}_{U_{j}}(\alpha_{j}g_{I}^{j}\oplus m_{j}g_{O/I}^{j}\oplus b_{j}^{n})}\right]

specify our joint random codebook distribution. In addition, throughout the rest of the proof we define

\displaystyle r_{U_{j}}^{n}(u_{j}^{n})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{p_{U_{j}}^{n}(u_{j}^{n})}{q_{U_{j}}^{n}(u_{j}^{n})},\mbox{ where }{q_{U_{j}}^{n}(u_{j}^{n})}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{n}}\mbox{ is the uniform distribution, and hence}

\displaystyle P\left(A_{j}(m_{j})=a_{j}\Big|C_{j}(m_{j})=\left(u_{j}^{n}(a_{j},m_{j}):a_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{j}}\right)\right)=\frac{r_{U_{j}}^{n}(u_{j}^{n}(a_{j},m_{j}))}{\sum_{\tilde{a}_{j}}r_{U_{j}}^{n}(u_{j}^{n}(\tilde{a}_{j},m_{j}))}.

(17)

We now derive an upper bound on the average error probability of a good code, by averaging the error probability of every code with respect to the above specified codebook distribution. Note that, upon receiving the quantum state
\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}
, Rx
1
prepares the auxiliary state
\outerproduct{0}{0}
, concatenates the same with the received state and measures this concatenated state via the square root measurement
[
31
,
34
]

\{\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}:(m_{1},s)\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\times\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}\}
on the combined state
\left(\rho^{Y_{1}}_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)
. Hence, the average error probability of the code is given by

\displaystyle\hskip-18.06749pt\mathbf{P}(\underline{e},\uline{\mu})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{s}\tr\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\otimes\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\otimes\mu_{m_{3}}^{Y_{3}}\right)\left(\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\end{aligned}\right\}.

Let
J
and
T
be operators on the quantum systems A and B, respectively, satisfying
0\leq J\leq I^{A}
and
0\leq T\leq I^{B}
. Then, observe that

\displaystyle I^{A}\otimes I^{B}-J\otimes T

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I^{A}-J)\otimes T+I^{A}\otimes(I^{B}-T)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(I^{A}-J)\otimes I^{B}+I^{A}\otimes I^{B}-(I^{A}\otimes T).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle I^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\otimes\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\otimes\mu_{m_{3}}^{Y_{3}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(I^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\otimes I^{Y_{2}}\otimes I^{Y_{3}}\right)+\left(I^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}-I^{Y_{1}}\otimes\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\otimes I^{Y_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(I^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}-I^{Y_{1}}\otimes I^{Y_{2}}\otimes\mu_{m_{3}}^{Y_{3}}\right).

Using the above inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\left(\underline{e},\uline{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{s}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}

(18)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu_{m_{3}}^{Y_{3}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}^{Y_{3}}\right\}

Rx
1
’s Error Analysis
: We begin by analyzing the first term in (
18
). We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{s}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{s}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{2}(m_{2})=a_{2}\right\}}\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{3}(m_{3})=a_{3}\right\}}

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{1}+t_{2},\mbox{ where}

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{s}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\left[\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{2}(m_{2})=a_{2}\right\}}\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{3}(m_{3})=a_{3}\right\}}-\frac{r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))}{\upsilon^{k_{2}}}\frac{r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}{\upsilon^{k_{3}}}\right]

(19)

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\},\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle t_{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{s}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}.

The last equality is obtained by adding and subtracting the product

\displaystyle\frac{r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))}{\upsilon^{k_{2}}}\frac{r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}{\upsilon^{k_{3}}}

(20)

which decomposes the expression into two terms.

Remark 4
.

We highlight the above technique of adding and subtracting the term in (
20
), which enables us to decompose the error into two terms, one which is analyzed through a covering
[
35
]
and the second through a conventional channel coding analysis. This enables us to break the independence and channel code analysis yields the right bound via the divergence. This approach essentially decouples the binning analysis and the channel coding analysis, thus leaving the channel coding analysis oblivious to the presence of binning.

Bounding
t_{1}
on the above in Prop.
4
below is performed by a covering argument
[
35
]
referred to therein as ‘cloud mixing’.

Proposition 4
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta
and sufficiently large
n
, we have
\mathbb{E}[t_{1}]\leq\epsilon
if

\displaystyle\frac{k_{j}}{n}\log(\Prime)>\log(\Prime)-H(U_{j}),\mbox{ for }j=2,3.

Proof.

See Appendix
C
for a proof.
∎

Our next task is to bound
t_{2}
on the above. To do this, we employ an alternate ‘proxy’ state. Specifically, for each codeword triplet, we substitute the original received state (
\rho_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}
) by a specific ‘tilted state’. Since the chosen tilted state is close in
\mathbb{L}_{1}-
norm to the original received state, the effect of this substitution on the error probability can be suppressed. Towards identifying this tilted state, we define a new tilting map

\mathcal{T}_{d_{1}^{n},d_{2}^{n},\eta}:\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{d_{1}^{n},d_{2}^{n},\eta}(\ket{h})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+2\eta^{2}}}\left(\ket{h}+\eta\ket{h}\otimes\ket{d_{1}^{n}}+\eta\ket{h}\otimes\ket{d_{2}^{n}}\right),

to tilt the state in all directions. As discussed earlier, this tilting is chosen carefully so as to ensure that the tilted state remains close to the original state in
\mathbb{L}_{1}
norm. Prop.
5
establishes this thereby guaranteeing that the two states induce approximately the same measurement outcome statistics. Let

\displaystyle\theta_{x_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n},u^{n},d_{2}^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{T}_{d_{1}^{n},d_{2}^{n},\eta}\left(\rho_{x_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right),

(21)

be the tilted state along all directions, where
\mathcal{T}_{d_{1}^{n},d_{2}^{n},\eta}
acts on each pure state in the mixture individually.

Proposition 5
.

For n sufficiently large, we have

\displaystyle\norm{\theta_{x_1^n,d_1^n,u^n,d_2^n} - \left( \rho_{x_1^n,u^n}^{Y_1} \otimes\ketbra{0}\right) }_{1}\leq 4\eta.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
D
.
∎

To bound the term
t_{2}
, we evaluate conditional expectation over
U_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})
and
U_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3})
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[t_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u_{2}^{n},u_{3}^{n}}\sum_{u^{n}}q_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n})q_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n})r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))

\displaystyle\mathds{1}{\{u^{n}=u_{2}^{n}\oplus u_{3}^{n}\}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{X_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}),f_{3}^{n}(u^{n}_{3})}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u_{2}^{n},u_{3}^{n}}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n})p_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n})\mathds{1}{\{u^{n}=u_{2}^{n}\oplus u_{3}^{n}\}}

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{X_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}),f_{3}^{n}(u^{n}_{3})}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u_{2}^{n},u_{3}^{n}}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})p_{U_{2}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}|u^{n})p_{U_{3}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}U_{2}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}|u^{n},u_{2}^{n})

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{X_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}),f_{3}^{n}(u^{n}_{3})}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\left(\rho_{X_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\leq}

\displaystyle t_{2.1}+t_{2.2},\mbox{ where }

\displaystyle t_{2.1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu^{Y_{1}}_{m_{1},s}\right)\theta_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(m_{1}),u^{n},D^{n}_{2}(s)}\right\},\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle t_{2.2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})\norm{\theta_{(X_1^n,D^n_1)(m_1),u^n,D^n_2(s)} - \left(\rho_{X_1^n(m_1),u^n}^{Y_1} \otimes\ketbra{0}\right) }_{1}.

In the above chain of equalities/inequalities, (a) is obtained by using
q_{U_{2}}^{n}(u^{n}_{2})r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n})=p_{U_{2}}^{n}(u^{n}_{2})
and
q_{U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n}_{3})r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n})=p_{U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n}_{3})
, (b) follows from

\displaystyle p_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n})p_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n})\mathds{1}\{u^{n}=u_{2}^{n}\oplus u_{3}^{n}\}=p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})p_{U_{2}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}|u^{n})p_{U_{3}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}U_{2}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}|u^{n},u_{2}^{n}),

(c) is obtained from the definition of
\rho^{Y_{1}}_{X_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),u^{n}}
in (
3
) and (d) follows from
substituting the state
\left(\rho_{X_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)
with the tilted state
\theta_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(m_{1}),u^{n},D^{n}_{2}(s)}
defined in (
21
). We simply bound the term
t_{2.2}
by
4\eta
using Prop.
5
. To upper-bound the term
t_{2.1}
, we employ Hayashi-Nagaoka inequality
[
28
]
. We obtain,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad t_{2.1}\leq 2\>t_{2.1.1}+4\left(t_{2.1.2}+t_{2.1.3}+t_{2.1.4}\right),\mbox{ where}

\displaystyle t_{2.1.1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})\tr\left\{\left(I-\gamma^{*}_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(m_{1}),u^{n},D_{2}^{n}(s)}\right)\theta_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(m_{1}),u^{n},D^{n}_{2}(s)}\right\},

\displaystyle t_{2.1.2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{\tilde{m}_{1}\neq m_{1}}\sum_{s}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})\tr\left\{\gamma^{*}_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(\tilde{m}_{1}),u^{n},D_{2}^{n}(s)}\theta_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(m_{1}),u^{n},D^{n}_{2}(s)}\right\},

\displaystyle t_{2.1.3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{s}\sum_{\tilde{s}\neq s}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})\tr\left\{\gamma^{*}_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(m_{1}),(U^{n},D_{2}^{n})(\tilde{s})}\theta_{(X_{1}^{n},D^{n}_{1})(m_{1}),u^{n},D^{n}_{2}(s)}\right\},

\displaystyle t_{2.1.4}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}}}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{\tilde{m}_{1}\neq m_{1}}\sum_{s}\sum_{\tilde{s}\neq s}\sum_{u^{n}}p_{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}}^{n}(u^{n})\tr\left\{\gamma^{*}_{(X_{1}^{n},D_{1}^{n})(\tilde{m}_{1}),(U^{n},D_{2}^{n})(\tilde{s})}\theta_{(X_{1}^{n},D_{1}^{n})(m_{1}),u^{n},D_{2}^{n}(s)}\right\}.

In the following propositions, we provide the rate constraints required to bound these error terms.

Proposition 6
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta,\eta>0
and sufficiently large
n
, we have
\mathbb{E}[t_{2.1.1}]\leq\epsilon
.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
E
.
∎

Proposition 7
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta,\eta>0
and sufficiently large
n
, we have
\sum_{i=2}^{4}\mathbb{E}[t_{2.1.i}]\leq\epsilon
if the following inequalities holds.

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(X_{1};Y_{1}|U_{2}\oplus U_{3}),

\displaystyle\frac{(k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}})}{n}\log(\Prime)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2}\oplus U_{3};Y_{1}|X_{1})+\log(\Prime)-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3}),\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle R_{1}+\frac{(k_{\mathsf{b}}+l_{\mathsf{b}})}{n}\log(\Prime)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(X_{1},U_{2}\oplus U_{3};Y_{1})+\log(\Prime)-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3}),

where all the mutual information quantities are computed with respect to the state

\displaystyle\rho^{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}U_{2}U_{3}X_{1}Y_{1}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{u,u_{2},u_{3},x_{1}}p_{X_{1}}(x_{1})p_{U_{2}}(u_{2})p_{U_{3}}(u_{3})\mathds{1}\{u=u_{2}\oplus u_{3}\}\outerproduct{u,u_2,u_3, x_1}{u,u_2,u_3, x_1}\otimes\rho_{x_{1},f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{3}(u_{3})}^{Y_{1}}.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
F
.
∎

This completes analysis of error at Rx
1
. We now proceed to analyzing the second and third terms in (
18
).

Rx
2
and
3
’s Error Analysis
: Since Rx
2
and
3
’s decoding POVMs and error probability terms are identical, we provide the analysis for Rx
2

-
the second term in (
18
). Observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\Gamma_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(m_{3}),m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{2}(m_{2})=a_{2}\right\}}\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{3}(m_{3})=a_{3}\right\}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\Gamma_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{1}+t_{2},\mbox{ where }

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\left[\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{2}(m_{2})=a_{2}\right\}}\mathds{1}{\left\{a_{3}(m_{3})=a_{3}\right\}}-\frac{r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))}{\upsilon^{k_{2}}}\frac{r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}{\upsilon^{k_{3}}}\right]

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I-\Gamma_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\},\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle t_{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))\tr\left\{\left(I-\Gamma_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\}.

The first inequality follows from
\mu_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\geq\Gamma_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}
, where

\displaystyle\hskip-18.06749pt\Gamma_{m_{2}}^{Y_{2}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\sum_{\widehat{m}_{2},\widehat{a}_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(\widehat{a}_{2},\widehat{m}_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(m_{2}),m_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\right)\left(\sum_{\widehat{m}_{2},\widehat{a}_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(\widehat{a}_{2},\widehat{m}_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

and the last equality follows from adding and subtracting the product

\displaystyle\frac{r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))}{\upsilon^{k_{2}}}\frac{r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}{\upsilon^{k_{3}}}.

We bound the term
t_{1}
using the same arguments as we used to bound the term in (
19
).

Proposition 8
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta
and sufficiently large

, we have
\mathbb{E}[t_{1}]\leq\epsilon
if

\displaystyle\frac{k_{j}}{n}\log(\Prime)>\log(\Prime)-H(U_{j}),\mbox{ for }j=2,3.

Proof.

The proof follows the same arguments as in Appendix
C
.
∎

To bound the term
t_{2}
, we employ Hayashi-Nagaoka inequality
[
28
]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad t_{2}\leq 2\>t_{2.1}+4\left(t_{2.2}+t_{2.3}\right),\mbox{ where}

\displaystyle t_{2.1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I-\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\right)\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle t_{2.2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\sum_{\tilde{a}_{2}\neq a_{2}}r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(\tilde{a}_{2},m_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle t_{2.3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\frac{1}{\upsilon^{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{a_{2},a_{3}}\sum_{\tilde{m}_{2}\neq m_{2}}\sum_{\tilde{a}_{2}}r_{U_{2}}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2}))r_{U_{3}}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\Pi^{Y_{2}}\Pi^{Y_{2}}_{u_{2}^{n}(\tilde{a}_{2},\tilde{m}_{2})}\Pi^{Y_{2}}\rho_{x_{1}^{n}(m_{1}),f_{2}^{n}(u_{2}^{n}(a_{2},m_{2})),f_{3}^{n}(u_{3}^{n}(a_{3},m_{3}))}^{Y_{2}}\right\}.

As is evident to an informed reader, the first term above
t_{2.1}
falls exponentially with the typicality parameter, placing no constraints on the rate. Each of the terms
t_{2.2},t_{2.3}
falls exponentially if the rate constraint stated in Prop.
10
is satisfied. We refer the reader to the corresponding appendices for a proof.

Proposition 9
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta
and sufficiently large
n
, we have
\mathbb{E}[t_{2.1}]\leq\epsilon
.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
G
.
∎

Proposition 10
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta
and sufficiently large
n
, we have
\mathbb{E}[t_{2.2}]+\mathbb{E}[t_{2.3}]\leq\epsilon
, if the following bounds hold

\displaystyle\frac{(k_{2}+l_{2})}{n}\log(\Prime)<I(U_{2};Y_{2})+\log(\Prime)-H(U_{2}),

where all the mutual information quantities are computed with respect to the state

\displaystyle\hskip-18.06749pt\rho^{U_{2}U_{3}X_{1}Y_{2}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{u_{2},u_{3},x_{1}}p_{X_{1}}(x_{1})p_{U_{2}}(u_{2})p_{U_{3}}(u_{3})\outerproduct{u_2,u_3,x_1}{u_2,u_3,x_1}\otimes\rho^{Y_{2}}_{x_{1},f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{3}(u_{3})}.

(22)

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
H
.
∎

We have thus bounded the error probability at Rx
2
- the second term in (
18
). As stated earlier, error analysis of Rx
3
- the third term in (
18
) is identical to that of the second term and is therefore omitted. This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 1
.

A rate-cost triple
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})
is achievable if there exists (i) a finite field
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{3}=\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
(ii) PMFs
p_{X_{1}}
,
p_{U_{2}X_{2}}
,
p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
such that
\mathbb{E}[\kappa(X_{j})]\leq\tau_{j}:j\in[3]
,

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\min\{0,H(U_{j})-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3}|Y_{1}):j=2,3\}+I(X_{1};Y_{1},U_{2}\oplus U_{3}),

\displaystyle R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(X_{j},U_{j};Y_{j}),

\displaystyle R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(U_{2}\oplus U_{3};Y_{1}|X_{1})-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3})+\min\{I(X_{2},Y_{2}|U_{2})+H(U_{2}),I(X_{3},Y_{3}|U_{3})+H(U_{3})\},\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle R_{1}+R_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle I(X_{j};Y_{j}|U_{j})+I(X_{1};U_{2}\oplus U_{3},Y_{1})+H(U_{j})-H(U_{2}\oplus U_{3}|Y_{1}):j=2,3\mbox{ holds},

with all information quantities being computed with respect to the state

\displaystyle\rho^{U_{2}\oplus U_{3}U_{2}U_{3}\underline{X}\underline{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{u,u_{2},u_{3},\underline{x}}p_{X_{1}}(x_{1})p_{U_{2}X_{2}}(u_{2},x_{2})p_{U_{3}X_{3}}(u_{3},x_{3})\mathds{1}{\{u=u_{2}\oplus u_{3}\}}\outerproduct{u,u_2,u_3,\ulinex}{u,u_2,u_3,\ulinex}\otimes\rho^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}_{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}.

Fig. 6:
The coding strategy involves five codes depicted in the grey box - two of them being NCCs built on the finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
and three unstructured IID code built on
\mathcal{X}_{j}
for
j\in[3]
.
Rx

, in addition to decoding into its code, decodes into the collection of ‘codewords’ in the orange dotted box. Specifically, the collection of the codewords in the orange dotted box is obtained by adding all pairs of codewords in the

Proof.

In comparison to the proof of Lemma
1
, the only differences (new elements) in this proof relate to users
2
and
3
. Specifically, instead of using a single coset code for encoding their message, Txs
2,3
split their message into two parts, encode one of the parts via a coset code, as in the proof of Lemma

, and the other via an unstructured IID code built over
\mathcal{X}_{j}:j=2,3
. See Fig.

. Rxs
2,3
employ a simultaneous decoder to decode into their codebooks. For this, we employ the technique of Fawzi et. al.
[

]
to perform simultaneous decoding as we are required to only decode into two codebooks. In the interest of brevity, we do not provide these analysis steps here. We refer the reader to
[

, Proof of Thm. 1]
provided in the context of a
3-
user quantum broadcast channel. The analysis steps therein can be straightforwardly adopted here.
∎

IV-A

Coset codes are strictly more efficient for Ex.
1
and Ex.
2

We demonstrate that the rate triple
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is achievable for Ex.
1
and
2
using coset codes. Thus proving that the latter is strictly more efficient for the additive, non-additive, non-commutative instances. We begin by stating a fact that will be used in the proofs of the following two propositions. Recall the notation defined in (
1
).

Fact 1
.

Suppose we have a state
\sigma^{AB}=\alpha\outerproduct{0}{0}^{A}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}^{B}+(1-\alpha)\outerproduct{1}{1}^{A}\otimes\outerproduct{v_{\varphi}}{v_{\varphi}}^{B}
, where
\alpha\in[0,1]
. It is easy to verify that

\displaystyle I(A;B)=h_{b}(f(1-\alpha)),\mbox{ where }f(t)=\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4t(1-t)\sin(\varphi)^{2}}}{2},\mbox{ for }t\in[0,1].

Proposition 11
.

Consider the
3-
CQIC in Ex.
1
under condition (
1
). The rate triple
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is achievable using coset codes as long as
C_{1}>\mathcal{C}_{1}+\max\{\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\}
.

Proof.

It can be verified that
\mathcal{C}_{j}=1-h_{b}(\delta_{j})
for
j=2,3
. From Fact
1
, we have
C_{1}=h_{b}\left(\frac{1+\cos(\varphi)}{2}\right)
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}=h_{b}\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\tau(1-\tau)\sin(\varphi)^{2}}}{2}\right)
.
We only need to identify an appropriate PMF
p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}X_{2}}p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
such that
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is achievable using coset codes. Let
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{3}=\mathcal{F}_{2}
. Define the PMF
p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}X_{2}}p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
on
\mathcal{U}_{2}\times\mathcal{U}_{3}\times\mathcal{\underline{X}}
such that (i)
p_{X_{1}}(1)=\tau
, and (ii)
p_{U_{j}X_{j}}(1,1)=1-p_{U_{j}X_{j}}(0,0)=\frac{1}{2}
for
j=2,3
.
It is straightforward to verify that the PMF
p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}X_{2}}p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
satisfies the input cost constraint and that the rate triple
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
satisfies the bounds in Thm.
1
.
∎

Proposition 12
.

Consider the
3-
CQIC in Ex.
2
under condition (
1
). The rate triple
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is achievable using coset codes, as long as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{3}<\vartheta,

where
\vartheta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\min\{h_{b}(\tau_{j})+(1-\tau_{1})(1-\tau_{2})\log\left((1-\tau_{1})(1-\tau_{2})\right)+(\tau_{1}*\tau_{2})\log(\tau_1*\tau_2)+\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\log(\tau_1 \tau_2)-h_{b}(f(\tau_{1}))+h_{b}(f(\tau_{1}*\beta)):j=2,3\}+h_{b}(f(\tau_{1}))
and
f(t)=\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4t(1-t)\sin(\varphi)^{2}}}{2}
, for
t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
.

Proof.

It can be verified that
\mathcal{C}_{j}=h_{b}(\tau_{j}*\delta_{j})-h_{b}(\delta_{j})
for
j=2,3
. From Fact
1
, we have
\mathcal{C}_{1}=h_{b}(f(\tau_{1}))
and
C_{1}=h_{b}(f(\tau_{1}*\beta))
, where
\beta=\tau_{2}+\tau_{3}-\tau_{2}\tau_{3}
and
f(t)=\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4t(1-t)\sin(\varphi)^{2}}}{2}
for
t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
.
We only need to identify an appropriate PMF
p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}X_{2}}p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
such that
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
is achievable using coset codes. Let
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{3}=\mathcal{F}_{3}
, and let
p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}X_{2}}p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
be a PMF defined on
\mathcal{U}_{2}\times\mathcal{U}_{3}\times\mathcal{\underline{X}}
, where (i)
p_{X_{1}}(1)=\tau_{1}
, and (ii)
p_{U_{j}X_{j}}(1,1)=1-p_{U_{j}X_{j}}(0,0)=\tau_{j},p_{U_{j}}(2)=0
for
j=2,3
.
It is straightforward to verify that the PMF
p_{X_{1}}p_{U_{2}X_{2}}p_{U_{3}X_{3}}
satisfies the input cost constraint and that the rate triple
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3})
satisfies the bounds in Thm.
1
, as long as
\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{3}<\vartheta
.
∎

V
Simultaneous Decoding of Coset Codes Over The
3-
CQIC

In Sec.
IV
, we provided a proof that includes all the new elements in a simplified setting, which was the focus of our first step. We now proceed to obtain a generalized inner bound. As discussed in Remark
1
, we now describe a general coding strategy that employs IID codes to decode uni-variate components and coset codes to decode bi-variate components of the interference at
each
Rx. We begin by introducing some notation.

Notation 2
.

In any context, when used in conjunction,
i,j,k
will denote distinct indices in
[3]
, hence
\{i,j,k\}=[3]
and we let

.

(Step II) only Rvs in the gray dashed box are non-trivial, with the rest trivial.

Tx
j
splits its message into
6
parts
-

W_{j}
, semi-private

Step II : Simultaneous Decoding of bi-variate Components

In this second step, we activate only the ‘bi-variate’
\underline{U}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(U_{1*},U_{2*},U_{3*})
and private parts

\underline{W}=\phi,\underline{V}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(V_{1*},V_{2*},V_{3*})=\phi

Theorem 2
.

Let
\hat{\alpha}_{S}\in[0,\infty)^{6}
be the set of all rate-cost vectors

holds for every

, where

Let

denote the convex closure of

. Then

.

9
codes employed in the coding strategy are depicted in the grey box above. The

Remark 5
.

Inner bound
\alpha_{S}
(i) subsumes
[
36
,
37
, Thm. 1]
, (ii) is the CQ analogue of
[
17
, Thm. 3]
, (iii) does not include a time-sharing Rv and includes the ‘don’t care’ inequalities
[
38
,
39
]
- (
26
), (
27
) with
A_{j}=\phi
. Thus,
\alpha_{S}
can be enlarged.

Discussion of the bounds
: We
explain
how (i) each bound arises and (ii) their role in driving error probability down. Given a message
m_{j}=(m_{ji},m_{jk},m_{jj})
with three parts, Tx
j
employs a likelihood encoder. On analyzing the error probability, the term corresponding to (
19
) will yield 15 source coding bounds
[
40
]
that are identical to those one would obtain if Tx
j
were attempting to find a triple of codewords jointly typical with respect to PMF
p_{U_{ji}U_{jk}X_{j}}
. (
23
), (
24
) are indeed these source coding bounds. To recognize this, throughout this discussion, we rename the Rvs
Z_{1}=U_{ji},Z_{2}=U_{jk},Z_{3}=X_{j}
, PMFs
r_{\underline{Z}}=r_{Z_{1}Z_{2}Z_{3}}=p_{U_{ji}U_{jk}X_{j}}
,
q_{\underline{Z}}=q_{Z_{1}Z_{2}Z_{3}}=\frac{p_{X_{k}}}{|\mathcal{U}_{ji}|\cdot|\mathcal{U}_{jk}|}
and bin sizes
B_{1}=S_{ji}-T_{ji},B_{2}=S_{jk}-T_{jk},B_{3}=K_{j}
. Since
c_{ji}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{u_{ji}^{n}(a_{ji},m_{ji}):a_{ji}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon_{i}}^{s_{ji}-t_{ji}},m_{ji}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon_{i}}^{t_{ji}}\},c_{jk}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{u_{jk}^{n}(a_{jk},m_{jk}):a_{jk}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon_{k}}^{s_{jk}-t_{jk}},m_{jk}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon_{k}}^{t_{jk}}\}
are random coset codes
[
36
]
with uniformly distributed codewords, and codewords of
X_{j}
picked IID with respect to
\prod p_{X_{j}}
, any triple of codewords is distributed with PMF
q_{\underline{Z}}^{n}
(Fig.
9
). Standard source coding bounds to find one triple to be jointly typical with respect to
r_{\underline{Z}}
among
2^{n(B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3})}
triples are
B_{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(A)}}{{>}}D(r_{Z_{A}|Z_{A^{C}}}||q_{Z_{A}|Z_{A^{C}}}|r_{Z_{A^{C}}})
, for every
A\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
, where
Z_{A}=(Z_{\iota}:\iota\in A)
and averaged divergence is as defined in
[
41
, Eqn. 2.4]
. The 7 bounds in (

built over
\mathcal{X}_{1}
. Rvs

and Rv

m_{11}

.

To analyze the
4-
user CQMAC, we adopt Sen’s
[
5
]
technique of
tilting, smoothing, and augmentation
. In this setting, there are
15=2^{4}-1
possible error events, each corresponding to a different subspace, and these subspaces can overlap in a nontrivial way. Sen’s
[
5
]
idea is to enlarge the space so that we can tilt each error subspace in an orthogonal direction and increase their separation. We also augment the space to ensure the smoothing property of the states obtained by averaging.
Following this approach, we define
14=(2^{4}-1)-1
tilting maps, one for each error subspace except the last, where all four messages
(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4})
are wrong and remain untilted. In what follows, we present this approach in detail. First, we define the new enlarged and augmented space. Next, we define the tilting maps.

For convenience, Table
II
summarizes the notation for the auxiliary spaces, tilting maps, and projectors used in the construction of the simultaneous decoding POVM.

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{S}_{d_{S}^{n},\eta}(\ket{h})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega(S,\eta)}}\left(\ket{h}+\eta^{|S|}\ket{h}\otimes\ket{d_{S}^{n}}\right),\mbox{ where }\Omega(S,\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}1+\eta^{2|S|}.

\displaystyle G^{S}_{\underline{z}^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\Pi_{z_{S^{c}}^{n}}\Pi_{\underline{z}^{n}}\Pi_{z_{S^{c}}^{n}},

(34)

Distribution of the random code:
As is standard in information theory, to derive an upper bound on the average error probability, we average over the ensemble of all codes.
The distribution of the random code is analogous to (
16
), that is employed in step I analysis involving
3
codes. This is naturally extended to the scenarios involving
9
codes, so in essence the unstructured codebooks are picked independently, and the coset codes are picked such that the larger of the codes in the same field contains the smaller one. For more elaboration, please look at the distribution (
16
) in step I.

Error Analysis:
Based on the exposition provided in Remark
4
,we recognize that our approach enables us to separate the binning analysis and the channel coding analysis, thereby breaking the independence. We therefore assume no binning in the following analysis.
As is standard in information theory, we derive an upper-bound on the probability of error. We decompose the average error probability of the
4-
user CQMAC (Fig.
11
) into two parts. This is done by employing, as suggested in
[
5
]
, an alternate ‘proxy’ state. Specifically, for each codeword quadruple, we substitute the original received state (
\xi_{\underline{Z}^{n}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}
) by a specific ‘tilted state’. Since the tilted state is chosen such that is close in
\mathbb{L}_{1}-
norm to the original received state (Prop.
13
), the effect of this substitution on the error probability can be suppressed. Towards identifying this tilted state, we define a new tilting map

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\mathcal{T}_{\underline{d}^{n},\eta}:\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{G}}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{e}}\mbox{ as }\mathcal{T}_{\underline{d}^{n},\eta}(\ket{h})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega(\eta)}}\left(\ket{h}+\sum_{S\subsetneq[4]}\eta^{|S|}\ket{h}\otimes\ket{d_{S}^{n}}\right),\mbox{ where }\Omega(\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}1+16\eta^{2}+36\eta^{4}+16\eta^{6}.

Let

\displaystyle\theta^{\otimes n}_{\underline{z}^{n}\underline{d}^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{d}^{n},\eta}\left\{\xi^{\otimes n}_{\underline{z}^{n}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right\}

be the tilted state with components in all appended auxiliary spaces. Here, the map
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{d}^{n},\eta}
is linear and acts on each pure state in a mixture individually.

Proposition 13
.

For
n
sufficiently large, we have

\displaystyle\norm{\theta_{\ulinez^{n}, \ulined^{n}}^{\otimes n} - \left(\xi_{\ulinez^{n}}^{\otimes n} \otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}\right) }_{1}\leq 12\eta.

(35)

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof in Appendix
D
.
∎

Having defined the alternate ‘proxy’ tilted state, we have

\displaystyle\hskip-18.06749pt\mathbf{P}(\underline{e},\mu)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu_{\underline{m}}\right)\left(\xi_{\underline{Z}}^{n}(\underline{m})\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\right)\right\}\leq t_{1}+t_{2},\mbox{ where}

\displaystyle\hskip-18.06749ptt_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\tr\left\{\left(I-\mu_{\underline{m}}\right)\theta_{\underline{Z}^{n}(\underline{m}),\underline{D}^{n}(\underline{m})}\right\},\mbox{ and }t_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\norm{\theta_{\ulineZ^n(\ulinem), \ulineD^n(\ulinem)}-\left(\xi_{\ulineZ^n(\ulinem)} \otimes\ketbra{0}\right)}_{1}.

The inequality follows from using the inequality
\tr\left(\Delta\rho\right)\leq\tr\left(\Delta\sigma\right)+\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{1}
, with
0\leq\Lambda,\rho,\sigma\leq I
.

We analyze the first term
t_{1}
, using the Hayashi–Nagaoka inequality
[
28
]
. This allows us to decompose
t_{1}
as

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826ptt_{1}=2\>t_{1.1}+4\sum_{S\subseteq[4]}t_{1.S},\mbox{ where}

\displaystyle t_{1.1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\tr\left\{(I-\gamma^{*}_{\underline{Z}^{n}(\underline{m}),\underline{D}^{n}(\underline{m})})\theta_{\underline{Z}^{n}(\underline{m}),\underline{D}^{n}(\underline{m})}\right\},\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle t_{1.S}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\underline{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{\tilde{m}_{S}\neq m_{S}}\tr\left\{\gamma^{*}_{Z_{S}^{n}(\tilde{m}_{S}),D^{n}_{S}(\tilde{m}_{S}),Z^{n}_{S^{c}}(m_{S^{c}}),D_{S^{c}}^{n}(m_{S^{c}})}\theta_{\underline{Z}^{n}(\underline{m}),\underline{D}^{n}(\underline{m})}\right\}.

The following propositions summarize all the rate constraints that result from bounding these error terms.

Proposition 14
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta,\eta>0
, and sufficiently large
n
, we have
\mathbb{E}[t_{1.1}]\leq\epsilon
.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
I
.
∎

Proposition 15
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and for all sufficiently small
\delta,\eta>0
, and sufficiently large
n
, we have
\mathbb{E}[t_{1.S}]\leq\epsilon,
if

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in S}\tilde{R}_{s}<I(Y;Z_{S}|Z_{S^{c}}),\mbox{ for }S\subseteq[4].

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
J
.
∎

Next, from Prop.
13
, we have for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and for
\eta=\epsilon^{\frac{1}{5}}
,
\mathbb{E}[t_{2}]\leq 12\>\epsilon^{\frac{1}{5}}
. The choice of
\eta
comes from ensuring
\mathbb{E}[t_{1.1}]\leq\epsilon
, for
n
sufficiently large, as shown in the proof of Prop.
14
.
Based on Remark
4
and our exposition in the proof in Sec.
IV
, specifically the separation of binning and channel coding, this proof is complete.
∎

V-B

Step III: Enlarging
\alpha_{S}
via Unstructured IID Codes to
\alpha_{US}

Incorporating the Han-Kobayashi technique
[
1
]
of decoding ‘uni-variate parts’ coded via unstructured IID codes, we characterize an inner bound
\alpha_{US}
that subsumes all currently known inner bounds. We provide a characterization of the same below. A proof of this inner bound is involved, but essentially it is a generalization of the proof provided for Lemma
1
and Thms.
1
,
2
. The code structure to prove achievability of the following inner bound is depicted in Fig.
12
. The reader is also referred to Fig.
7
and the associated discussion found at the beginning of Sec.
V
.

Fig. 12:
15
codes employed in the proof of Thm.
3
. Row
j
depicts the codes of Tx, Rx
j
. The
U_{ji}:ji\in\llbracket 3\rrbracket
are coset codes built over finite fields. Codes with the same color are built over the same finite field, and the smaller of the two is a sub-coset of the larger. The black codes are built over auxiliary finite sets
\mathcal{V}_{ji}:ji\in\llbracket 3\rrbracket
using the conventional IID random code structure. The orange codes are conventional IID random codes built over input sets
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}
and
\mathcal{X}_{3}
.

Notation 4
.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{j*}=\mathcal{V}_{ji}\times\mathcal{V}_{jk}
and
\mathcal{U}_{j*}=\mathcal{U}_{ji}\times\mathcal{U}_{jk}
. For any set
A
,
A^{c}
denotes the complement of
A
with the underlying universe set being clear from context.

\displaystyle\beta_{C_{j}}+S_{A_{j}}

u_{ij}\oplus u_{ik}=u_{i}^{\oplus}\end{subarray}\right\}}\outerproduct{\ulinev~\ulineu~ u_{1}^{\oplus}~ u_{2}^{\oplus}~ u_{3}^{\oplus}~ \ulinex}{\ulinev~\ulineu~ u_{1}^{\oplus}~ u_{2}^{\oplus}~ u_{3}^{\oplus}~ \ulinex}\otimes\rho_{\underline{x}}.

Remark 6
.

The inner bound characterized above subsumes the current known largest inner bound for the
3-
CQIC achievable using unstructured IID codes. Indeed, choosing
U_{ji}=\phi
for
(i,j)\in\llbracket 3\rrbracket
we recover the latter inner bound.

Remark 7
.

The above inner bound is characterized for a
3-
CQIC. We can extend this via the standard technique of regularization
[
31
, Sec. 20.1, Eq. 20.7]
to characterize an inner bound to the bit communicating capacity region of a general
3-
user quantum interference channel. We omit a mathematical characterization of this inner bound in the interest of brevity.
